marketplace sales are 30%.

Does the 10% Include Payment Processing?

No. Gumroad's fee help says the 10% + $0.50 on sales
made on Gumroad's website does not include credit card
processing or PayPal fees. On Latuos, the 2% platform
fee and Stripe processing are separate too.

What Is Gumroad's Payout Schedule?

Gumroad says sellers can choose daily, weekly, monthly,
or quarterly payouts, but all sales have a minimum 7 day
holding period and payouts include sales made through
the previous Friday. Daily payouts and instant payouts
are limited to eligible US sellers, and instant payouts
carry a 3% fee.

Is Gumroad Merchant of Record?

Yes. Gumroad says it now acts as the Merchant of Record
for all sales and automatically handles sales tax
collection and remittance worldwide. That also means
Gumroad's fee comparison includes a payout model where
Gumroad controls payout eligibility and timing.

Does Gumroad charge monthly fees?

No. Gumroad says there are no monthly payments or other
hidden charges.

Is Gumroad free to start?

Gumroad says there are no monthly payments or other
hidden charges, so creators can start without a monthly
subscription. Sellers still pay Gumroad's per-sale fees
when they make sales.

Does Gumroad's 30% Discover fee include processing?

Yes. Gumroad's fee help says sales made through
Gumroad's marketplace are charged a flat 30% fee and
that this includes all processing fees.

Who can get listed on Gumroad Discover?

Gumroad says Discover eligibility requires completed
payout settings, at least $100 USD from genuine sales,
and risk-team verification. It also says a product needs
at least one successful sale, a category, and ratings
enabled to be listed, and Discover-listed products are
automatically opted into Gumroad's affiliate program.

Does Gumroad handle VAT and sales tax for sellers?

Gumroad says it now acts as the Merchant of Record for
creator sales and handles the tax obligations tied to
that role. Its pricing page also says creators do not
need to manage VAT, GST, or other international tax
requirements, and that Gumroad collects the appropriate
taxes where it has merchant-of-record tax obligations.

Can you use Gumroad for memberships or SaaS
subscriptions?

Yes. Gumroad's pricing FAQ says creators can use Gumroad
for memberships, including podcasts, online courses, and
SaaS subscriptions.

Does Gumroad provide a reseller certificate?

Yes. Gumroad's pricing FAQ says some creators may need
to download and save Gumroad's reseller certificate so
they can report Gumroad sales as resale transactions to
their own tax authority.

What can and can't you sell on Gumroad?

Gumroad says creators can sell digital products,
e-books, courses, tutorials, memberships, and certain
service products. Its service-product help says calls,
commissions, and tip-style products are limited to
eligible accounts under Gumroad's current service rules.
Gumroad also prohibits categories such as illegal
products or services, securities and crypto products,
credit repair, and sexually explicit content.
Non-explicit NSFW work must be tagged and is only shown
to customers who opt in.

By Victor Organo, Founder of Latuos &middot; Updated July 6, 2026

Quick Answer

Ko-fi charges 0% on one-time tips on Free. Memberships,
monthly support, shop sales, and commissions are 5%, while
new creators start on Contributor by default, which applies
5% to all payment types. Gold costs $12/month and removes
Ko-fi service fees. Payment processor fees still apply.

Ko-fi does not use one flat fee across every monetization
path. Free, Contributor, and Gold change what you pay, and
for paid digital sales the most relevant number is usually
5% unless Gold already makes sense for your setup.

On Ko-fi Free, one-time tips stay 0%, while memberships,
monthly support, shop sales, and commissions are 5%.
Contributor applies 5% across all payments, including
one-time tips, and Ko-fi says new creators start there by
default unless they opt out. Gold costs $12/month and
removes Ko-fi service fees.

For digital sellers comparing Ko-fi with Latuos, the real
question is not just percentage. It is whether you want a
mixed creator page built around tips, memberships, shop
sales, and commissions, or a storefront-first setup for
straightforward digital-product sales with hosted checkout,
secure delivery, and your own Stripe account at the center.

The current Latuos platform fee is 2% of each completed
sale. Latuos does not charge a monthly subscription. The

connection, and digital delivery layer.

That fee covers hosted storefronts and product pages,
one-time checkout, secure file and link delivery, buyer
re-download access, pay-what-you-want pricing, discount

What Ko-fi Fees Actually Are

Ko-fi's own pricing and help pages split the answer across
Free, Contributor, and Gold. On Free, one-time tips can
stay at 0%, but paid monetization like shop sales,
memberships, monthly support, and commissions is 5%.
Contributor keeps the account at $0/month but applies 5%
across all payments, including tips.

That is why creators often talk past each other about
Ko-fi pricing. One creator may be quoting the one-time 0%
tip path on Free, while another is quoting Contributor or
the 5% service-fee path for paid monetization. Both can be
describing official Ko-fi pricing, but they are not
describing the same setup.

Gold is still available at $12/month with 0% Ko-fi service
fees, but Ko-fi's current help content puts more emphasis
on the Free and Contributor paths. For digital sellers,
that means the most relevant comparison against Latuos is
usually 5% on Ko-fi Free or Contributor versus 2% on
Latuos, unless Gold already makes sense for the business.

How Ko-fi Pays Creators

Ko-fi is a direct-payments platform. Creators connect
PayPal, Stripe, or both, and supporters pay straight into
that account.

Ko-fi says creators get paid instantly and directly into
their own PayPal or Stripe account, with no waiting and no
platform-held balance. That matters when comparing fee
pages, because Ko-fi's platform fees sit on top of the
payment processor while the creator still receives funds
directly.

When Ko-fi Is Cheaper

Ko-fi is clearly cheaper if one-time tips are the main
business and the creator is using the Free setup rather
than default Contributor. Latuos is not a tip-jar product,
so that 0% one-time-tip path is a real Ko-fi advantage.

For digital downloads, memberships, and commissions, the
more relevant Ko-fi number is usually 5% on Free or
Contributor. Ko-fi can still be cheaper once Gold makes
sense. At enough monthly volume, a flat $12/month can beat
a 2% variable fee on platform-fee math alone.

Ko-fi is also the stronger fit when the creator wants one
page to combine tips, memberships, shop items,
commissions, posts, and supporter-style monetization
instead of a simpler storefront.

Ko-fi Gold Break-even Versus Latuos

Based on official pricing, Gold costs $12/month with 0%
Ko-fi service fees. On platform-fee math alone, that
reaches parity with Latuos's 2% fee at about $600 in
monthly sales because 2% of $600 is $12.

At $100/month in sales, Latuos's 2% platform fee is about
$2. At $250/month, it is about $5. At $600/month, it
reaches $12. Above that level, Gold can be cheaper on
platform-fee math if Ko-fi's broader creator model already
fits the business.

That comparison isolates platform fees only. PayPal or
Stripe processing fees sit on top either way.

Below that level, many smaller digital sellers still
prefer not to add another monthly subscription. That is
where Latuos's simpler no-monthly-fee structure is easier
to justify.

When Latuos Is the Better Fit

Latuos is the better fit when the seller mainly wants to
sell one-time digital products and does not need tips,
commissions, memberships, or mixed creator monetization on
the same page.

The product is narrower by design. That is useful when the
goal is a storefront-first setup with hosted checkout,
post-purchase delivery, product-focused pages, and simpler
fee math rather than a creator page with many monetization
paths competing for attention.

If shop sales are the main use case and tips or
memberships are not, Latuos keeps the answer simpler: a 2%
platform fee with no monthly plan choice layered on top.

What the 2% on Latuos Actually Covers

If you mainly sell downloads, templates, guides, ebooks,
digital art, or small bundles, Latuos is not just a lower
monthly-commitment option. It is a focused product layer
for digital sales on top of Stripe.

Hosted storefronts and product pages with shareable
URLs, branding, and social-preview support.

One-time digital checkout with instant delivery after
successful payment.

Secure file and link delivery, including support for
downloadable files, Notion links, download limits, and
buyer re-download access.

Selling tools like pay-what-you-want pricing, discount
codes, optional thank-you messages, and basic seller
analytics.

Extra protection features like signed private file
access and optional PDF stamping for anti-piracy use
cases.

That is the strongest Latuos argument on this page: if
your business is primarily digital-product sales, the 2%
is paying for a focused storefront and delivery workflow,
not for a broader creator-support stack you may not need.

Storefront-first Versus Mixed Creator Monetization

Ko-fi is broader. It is designed to help creators collect
support, run memberships, sell products, and take
commissions from one creator page while still paying them
directly through PayPal or Stripe.

Latuos is narrower. It is designed for hosted digital
product checkout and delivery, with a simpler direct-sale
story.

That means this is not really a "which platform is better"
comparison. It is a question of whether the seller wants a
mixed creator page or a storefront-first digital sales
tool.

Best For

Sellers who mainly want one-time digital-product sales,
simple fee math, no monthly subscription, and a cleaner
storefront for products like templates, printables,
guides, digital art, ebooks, and small bundles.

Not the Best Fit For

Creators whose business is built around tips, memberships,
commissions, supporter-only content, or a broader creator
page where Ko-fi's mixed monetization tools are the main
draw.

Frequently Asked Questions

What Are Ko-fi Fees in 2026?

Ko-fi charges 0% on one-time tips on Free. Memberships,
monthly support, shop sales, and commissions are 5%,
while Contributor applies 5% to all payment types. Gold
costs $12/month and removes Ko-fi service fees. Payment
processor fees still apply.

What Are Ko-fi Shop Fees for Digital Downloads?

Digital downloads sold through Ko-fi Shop follow Ko-fi's
paid-sales pricing: 5% on Free, 5% on Contributor, and
0% on Gold. PayPal or Stripe processing fees are
separate.

How Do Ko-fi Commission Fees Work?

Ko-fi Commissions are available to everyone. Ko-fi
charges 5% on commission payments for Free or
Contributor creators, while Gold creators pay 0% service
fees. Payment processor fees still apply, and payments
go directly to the creator's PayPal or Stripe account.

Does Ko-fi Hold Your Money or Pay You Directly?

Ko-fi is a direct-payments platform. Creators connect
PayPal, Stripe, or both, and Ko-fi says creators get
paid instantly and directly into their own account.
Ko-fi also says it does not hold the creator's money.

Does Ko-fi Take 0% on Everything?

No. Ko-fi's 0% path applies to one-time tips on Free,
while shop sales, memberships, monthly support, and
commissions are 5%. Contributor also applies 5% to
one-time tips.

When Is Ko-fi Gold Cheaper Than Latuos?

On platform-fee math alone, Gold crosses Latuos's 2%
platform fee at about $600/month in sales because 2% of
$600 is $12. Above that level, Gold can be cheaper if
Ko-fi's broader creator model already fits the business.

Do New Ko-fi Creators Start on Contributor?

Yes. Ko-fi says new creators now start with Contributor
status enabled by default, and they can opt out anytime.

When Is Latuos the Simpler Fit Than Ko-fi?

Latuos is the simpler fit when you mainly sell one-time
digital products and want a storefront-first setup with
no monthly fee, rather than tips, memberships,
commissions, and mixed creator monetization on one page.

What Does Latuos's 2% Fee Actually Cover?

Latuos's 2% platform fee covers the hosted storefront,
product pages, Stripe checkout connection, secure file
and link delivery, buyer re-download access,
pay-what-you-want pricing, discount codes, and seller
tools like customer lists and basic sales analytics.
Stripe processing is separate, and VAT may apply to
Latuos platform fees where required.

Does Latuos Have Any Monthly Fees?

No. Latuos does not charge a monthly subscription.
Sellers pay the 2% Latuos platform fee only when a sale
is completed, plus separate Stripe processing fees.

Does Latuos's 2% Fee Include Stripe Processing?

No. The 2% is the Latuos platform fee only. Stripe
processing is charged separately by Stripe in the
seller's connected Stripe account. In a common US
online-card example, a $25 sale has a $0.50 Latuos fee
plus about $1.03 in Stripe processing, before VAT,
taxes, refunds, chargebacks, currency conversion, and
non-card payment methods.

Who Handles Refunds and Chargebacks on Latuos?

The seller handles refunds, disputes, and chargebacks
through their own Stripe account because the seller
remains merchant of record. Latuos provides the
storefront and delivery layer and does not hold a seller
balance or run a separate payout queue.

Gumroad explainer for digital sellers

What is Gumroad? How it works for digital sellers in 2026

By Victor Organo, Founder of Latuos &middot; Updated June 26, 2026

Gumroad is an online platform for selling digital products,
memberships, and other direct offers. It gives creators a
simple way to publish products and let Gumroad handle the
storefront, checkout, and merchant-of-record layer in one
place.

Core use

Digital products

Also used for memberships and other direct offers.

Selling model

All-in-one

Gumroad handles product pages, checkout, and basic
delivery in one platform.

Merchant of record

Since Jan 1, 2025

Gumroad says it handles merchant-of-record
responsibilities on its own pricing page.

What Gumroad does well

Gumroad is built to help creators start selling quickly.
You can list a digital product, collect payment, and let
Gumroad handle checkout, basic delivery, and payout flow.

That simplicity is why Gumroad shows up so often in
creator conversations. It can be a practical starting
point for ebooks, templates, downloads, memberships, and
lightweight direct offers.

Best short answer

Gumroad is easiest to justify when the priority is getting a
product live fast, not owning every part of checkout and
payout infrastructure.

How Gumroad works for sellers

1. List a product

Create a product page for a digital download, membership,
or other creator offer.

2. Gumroad handles checkout

Buyers pay on Gumroad's infrastructure rather than through
the seller's own Stripe account.

3. Gumroad manages the payout flow

Because Gumroad acts as merchant of record, checkout, tax
handling, and payout flow run through Gumroad rather than
through the seller's own Stripe account directly.

What you can sell on Gumroad

Gumroad says creators use it for digital products, e-books,
courses, tutorials, and memberships. That makes it a fit for
straightforward digital offers that can live inside one
hosted storefront and checkout flow.

Gumroad also supports some service products. Its help docs
currently indicate accounts must be at least 30 days old and
have received at least one payout before they can offer
calls, commissioned work, and tips. At the same time,
Gumroad generally does not allow reselling products you did
not create and restricts certain prohibited or high-risk
categories, so it is not a fit for every kind of offer.

What buying on Gumroad looks like

On the buyer side, Gumroad keeps the flow simple. Customers
can buy with or without a Gumroad account.

If they create an account, they can access purchases later in
the Gumroad Library. If they check out without an account,
they can still use the receipt link Gumroad emails after
purchase, which includes the download link and any
product-specific access details.

Where to find Gumroad's current pricing

Gumroad's public pricing currently lists 10% + $0.50 on direct
and profile sales and 30% on Discover sales. For the detailed
fee breakdown and current caveats, see
Gumroad Fees Explained .

This page stays focused on what Gumroad is and who it fits.
The fee page is the better place for detailed pricing
questions.

Pricing checked against Gumroad's official pricing page in
June 2026. Rates can change.

Why merchant of record matters

Gumroad says it has been merchant of record since January 1,
2025. In practice, that means Gumroad handles the checkout
and tax layer itself instead of the seller running
everything through their own Stripe account directly.

Some creators prefer that because it reduces setup overhead.
Others eventually want a selling stack where payments and
operations are tied more directly to their own account as
the business grows.

Direct Stripe contrast

On Latuos, the seller connects Stripe directly and keeps
payments tied to their own Stripe account. That is a
different operating model from Gumroad's merchant-of-record
setup.

Who Gumroad is best for

Gumroad is best for creators who want to launch quickly,
sell standard digital products or memberships, and keep the
stack simple.

It can be a reasonable fit when the main goal is fast setup,
hosted checkout, and basic delivery without building a more
customized storefront or payment setup first.

When sellers start looking for alternatives

Sellers usually start looking elsewhere when they want more
control over checkout, payments, branding, or how the
selling stack connects to the rest of their business.

That is where comparison pages like
Gumroad vs Latuos ,
Gumroad vs Stripe Direct , and
Best Gumroad Alternatives in 2026
become more useful than a neutral definition, especially if
you want a broader shortlist built around verified pricing
and payout tradeoffs.

Seller safety and platform risk guide

Is Gumroad Safe? What Creators Need to Know in 2026

By Victor Organo, Founder of Latuos &middot; Updated June 16, 2026

Quick Answer

Gumroad is a legitimate platform, and buyer payment safety
is different from seller operating risk. For sellers, the
real questions are payout rules, account review, product
policy, support, and platform dependency.

Buyers mostly need to know whether checkout and file
delivery are legitimate. Sellers need to know who controls
the transaction, when payouts are eligible, what happens
during review, and how much of the business depends on one
platform account.

Safety is not binary. Gumroad works well for many creators,
but it is still a platform dependency. A seller-owned Stripe
setup can reduce one layer of storefront dependency because
the payment account belongs to the seller rather than the
platform.

If you are really asking whether Gumroad is legit, the
answer is yes. The seller-side question is whether the
platform model still matches what you want for payouts,
fees, and operational control.

Is Gumroad Safe for Buyers?

Gumroad is a real platform used for digital-product
checkout and delivery. Gumroad's buyer help docs describe
payment safety, purchase access, and support paths for
buyer issues.

Buyer safety does not answer the whole seller question. A
buyer can complete a purchase safely while the seller
still needs to think about fees, payout rules, refund
handling, account review, and product-policy limits.

Is Gumroad Safe for Sellers?

Gumroad can be a reasonable starting point for sellers who
want a hosted checkout and built-in delivery. The tradeoff
is that checkout, payout timing, account review, product
eligibility, and dispute handling run through Gumroad's
system.

Seller safety improves when the seller understands those
rules before revenue depends on the platform. Review the
payout minimum, payout timing, product restrictions, and
account-review process before treating any platform as the
only place your business can operate.

Seller Risks to Understand Before Relying on Gumroad

The main seller risks are operational rather than simple
fraud questions: payout eligibility, account review,
prohibited-product rules, refund and dispute handling, and
support response time. Public reviews and forum posts are
mixed, so treat them as signals to investigate rather than
proof that every seller will have the same experience.

Gumroad's current payout help page says sellers need at
least $100 USD in balance before payout, with some higher
local-currency minimums. That kind of rule matters if you
sell low-priced products or depend on frequent payouts.

How to Sell on Gumroad More Safely

Keep local copies of product files, maintain your own
customer communication channel, understand product
restrictions before listing, and export records you may
need for support, refunds, or tax review.

Also understand payout mechanics before a delay happens.
The
Gumroad payout holds guide
covers the payout side, and the
Gumroad suspension guide
covers account-review and recovery steps.

Safety Means More Than Fraud Protection

Seller safety is about fees, payout dependence, account
risk, refund control, and what happens when support or
compliance issues interrupt normal operations. That is one
reason the seller-owned Stripe model matters in this
comparison.

The fee calculator does not measure safety, but it shows
what you are paying for Gumroad's platform-managed
checkout and payout model versus a direct-payout setup.

Buyer Safety and Seller Safety Are Different Questions

A platform can be safe for buyers and still create
operational risk for sellers. Buyer safety is about
payments, fraud, and receiving the product. Seller safety
is about fees, payout control, account dependency, and
what happens during disputes or compliance reviews.

The distinction matters because buyer safety and seller
risk are different questions. Some sellers choose a
lower-dependency setup even if buyers are comfortable
purchasing on larger platforms.

What Makes a Safer Seller Setup

A lower-fee structure helps, but the bigger change is
keeping the payment account in the seller&#x27;s own name
and dashboard.

That can reduce one major layer of platform dependency.

Who this is best for

- Sellers trying to separate buyer safety from seller
operating risk.

- Sellers comparing quick setup against payout
independence.

- Sellers deciding whether Gumroad is still the right fit
as sales become meaningful.

Who this is not for

- Sellers who only want a yes-or-no answer about fraud
safety.

- Sellers who are comfortable with platform-managed payout
control.

- Sellers looking for legal or compliance advice about one
specific case.

Is Gumroad Legit for Sellers, and Safe for What Exactly?

Gumroad can be safe for quick setup. Buyers can pay, files
can be delivered, and the platform is familiar enough that
many sellers treat it as a default option early on. That
is real value, especially when the goal is shipping fast.

The seller-safety question is different. Once fees, payout
dependence, and account-level risk matter more, the
platform model starts to matter more than the buyer-facing
checkout experience. A platform can be perfectly usable
for buyers and still be less attractive for sellers
optimizing for payout independence and lower dependency.

The honest answer is conditional: Gumroad can work well
for some early creators, but it may be less ideal for
sellers who now care more about payment visibility and
payout workflow than convenience. The useful comparison is
not buyer trust alone. It is what the seller is trying to
optimize at this stage of the business.

Refunds and disputes show the seller-safety difference

Refunds and disputes matter because they reveal which
account charged the customer, where funds arrive first,
and which workflow handles payout timing when something
needs to be reversed. In Gumroad's model, those events run
through the platform. In Stripe-direct setups, the seller
sees those events in their own Stripe account and manages
them there. That does not make one model universally
safer; it makes the seller-risk tradeoff much clearer.

Run your price through the Fee Calculator to compare
supported platform fees side by side.

Frequently Asked Questions

Is Gumroad safe for buyers?

Gumroad is a legitimate platform, and its help docs
describe payment safety, purchase access, and support
options for buyers. Buyer safety is different from
seller operating risk.

Is Gumroad safe for sellers?

Gumroad can be a reasonable seller platform, but sellers
should understand payout rules, account review,
product-policy limits, and support dependency before
relying on it for meaningful revenue.

Why do some sellers worry about Gumroad payouts?

Gumroad's payout docs include eligibility rules, a
minimum payout threshold, account review, and payout
timing. Those mechanics are normal to understand before
depending on any platform.

Is Any Platform Completely Risk-Free?

No. Every platform is a dependency. The risk question is
how much of your business disappears if the platform
relationship changes. Owning the payment account can
reduce that exposure.

content:
Gumroad vs Stripe Direct: 10% vs 2.9% Per Sale

By Victor Organo, Founder of Latuos &middot; Updated July 4, 2026

Quick Answer

Gumroad is merchant of record and bundles tax handling with
platform-set payout rules. Selling direct on Stripe keeps
the payment account with the seller, which usually lowers US
direct-sale payment-layer fees but leaves more operating
responsibility with the business.

Creators searching for Gumroad vs Stripe are usually
deciding between convenience and ownership. Gumroad gives
you one system for checkout, tax handling, and payout
handling. Selling direct on Stripe usually lowers the
payment-layer fee on US direct sales, but it also means the
seller owns more of the operating setup.

This page uses US public pricing and official source pages
checked in June 2026. The cleanest way to compare the two is
by separating fee math, merchant-of-record status, payout
control, and setup burden rather than treating them as the
same product with different logos.

What is the difference between using Stripe directly and
Gumroad?

Question

Gumroad

Selling direct on Stripe

Direct-sale fees

Gumroad fee help says sales made on Gumroad's
website are 10% + $0.50 before card processing or
PayPal fees

Stripe's published US pricing starts at 2.9% + 30c
per successful domestic card charge

Merchant of record

Gumroad says it has been the merchant of record
since January 1, 2025

The seller stays merchant of record unless they
add a separate merchant-of-record product such as
Managed Payments

Payout control

Gumroad says sellers can choose daily, weekly,
monthly, or quarterly payouts, but sales have a
minimum 7-day holding period before payout
eligibility

Charges run on the seller's Stripe account, so
balances and payout settings stay in Stripe

Tax handling

Gumroad says it handles sales tax, VAT, and GST
where it has merchant-of-record obligations

The seller handles tax setup by default; Stripe's
Managed Payments is a separate add-on

Setup burden

Lower setup burden because Gumroad bundles
checkout, delivery, tax handling, and payout flow

Stripe Checkout is included at no extra charge,
but the seller still needs the surrounding
storefront, delivery, and business setup

Marketplace reach

Gumroad Discover exists, and Gumroad says
marketplace sales are charged a flat 30% fee

No built-in marketplace or discovery layer

How does Gumroad's merchant-of-record model work?

Gumroad packages checkout, delivery, tax handling, and
payouts into one system. For direct sales made on
Gumroad's website, Gumroad's fee help says the fee is 10%
+ $0.50 before separate card processing or PayPal fees.
For Discover marketplace sales, Gumroad says the fee is a
flat 30% and includes processing.

Gumroad's pricing page also says it has been the merchant
of record since January 1, 2025. That matters because
Gumroad then becomes the party handling sales tax
collection and remittance where it has those obligations,
rather than leaving that burden on the creator.

That convenience also means Gumroad sets the payout rules
and eligibility conditions. Gumroad's payout help says
sellers can choose daily, weekly, monthly, or quarterly
payouts, but all sales have a minimum 7-day holding period
before they become eligible for payout.

How does selling direct on Stripe keep the payment account
with the seller?

Selling direct on Stripe means the payment account belongs
to the seller. Stripe's direct-charges documentation says
you create charges on the connected account, and Stripe's
US pricing starts at 2.9% + 30c for successful domestic
card charges.

Stripe Checkout itself is included at no additional charge
on standard payments pricing, so the gap is not that
Stripe lacks a checkout product. The gap is that Stripe is
still only part of the stack. You still need storefront,
delivery, catalog, support flow, and the business
decisions that Gumroad bundles for you.

It also changes the merchant-of-record question. By
default, the seller keeps that role. If you want Stripe to
take that on, Stripe sells Managed Payments as a separate
merchant-of-record product at an added 3.5% fee per
successful transaction on top of standard payment fees.

Why the fee gap is only part of the decision

On direct-sale fee math alone, Stripe's published US
pricing is lower than Gumroad's direct-sale fee. That is
real, and it matters most for sellers who already bring
their own traffic.

But the comparison stops being honest if it treats Stripe
like a full Gumroad replacement by itself. Gumroad is not
just a processor markup. Gumroad also bundles delivery,
merchant-of-record tax handling, and Gumroad-managed
payouts into that structure.

That is why the real decision is not only about which
checkout fee looks lower. It is about whether you want the
payment relationship and payout visibility inside your own
Stripe account badly enough to own more of the surrounding
setup.

Can I keep my own Stripe account without building the full
stack myself?

The middle path is using a Stripe-first platform that
keeps payments tied to the seller's own Stripe account but
does not force the seller to build the entire
storefront-and-delivery stack alone. That is the role
Latuos is built for.

On
Latuos pricing , Latuos says it charges a 2% platform fee per sale with
no monthly fees, while Stripe processing applies
separately. Latuos also says sellers stay merchant of
record and receive payouts directly through their
connected Stripe account.

That makes it a better fit when the seller wants direct
Stripe payouts, hosted checkout, and digital delivery, but
does not want Gumroad setting payout rules or does not
want to assemble the storefront and delivery stack from
scratch.

When Gumroad still makes sense

Gumroad still makes sense when simplicity is the priority
and the seller wants one system for checkout, tax
handling, and payout handling.

It is also the cleaner fit if Gumroad Discover matters to
your sales model, because that is a marketplace path that
direct Stripe setups do not replace.

If the seller would rather outsource merchant-of-record
tax obligations than keep them in-house, Gumroad's model
is doing real work for that fee.

When selling direct on Stripe makes more sense

Selling direct on Stripe makes more sense when the seller
already brings most of their own traffic and wants the
payment account, balance visibility, and payout settings
to live in Stripe rather than inside a platform wallet.

It is usually the better long-term fit when fee drag on
direct sales matters more than merchant-of-record
outsourcing, or when the seller wants tighter visibility
into refunds, disputes, and payout timing.

The tradeoff is operational. The seller gets more direct
control, but they also keep more of the setup and merchant
responsibilities unless they add other tools or services
around Stripe.

Who this comparison is best for

Sellers comparing seller-owned Stripe payouts against a
merchant-of-record platform

Creators who already have direct traffic and want the
fee and payout tradeoff explained plainly

Operators deciding whether Gumroad convenience is still
worth the higher direct-sale take rate

Why payout visibility changes the decision

Refunds and disputes are where the structural difference
becomes obvious. In Gumroad's model, the same platform
that runs checkout also controls the payout layer. In a
direct Stripe setup, the payment events live in the
seller's own Stripe account. That does not remove payment
risk, but it does change who sees the balance, who sees
the payout settings, and where the seller works when
something unusual happens.

Frequently Asked Questions

Is Stripe cheaper than Gumroad?

On official US public pricing, Stripe's standard online
card fee is lower than Gumroad's direct-sale fee.
Gumroad says sales made on Gumroad's website are charged
10% + $0.50 before card processing or PayPal fees, while
Stripe's US pricing starts at 2.9% + 30 cents per
successful domestic card charge. That does not make it a
perfect apples-to-apples comparison because Gumroad also
bundles checkout, delivery, and merchant-of-record tax
handling.

Who is the merchant of record on Gumroad?

Gumroad says it has been the merchant of record since
January 1, 2025 and handles sales tax collection and
remittance worldwide where it has those obligations.

Can I use Stripe instead of Gumroad?

Yes. Stripe supports direct charges on connected
accounts, so you can sell through your own Stripe
account instead of Gumroad. The tradeoff is that you
still need the surrounding setup for storefront,
delivery, and the merchant-of-record responsibilities
you keep unless you add a separate merchant-of-record
solution.

What is the difference between using Stripe directly and
Gumroad?

Using Stripe directly keeps the payment account, payout
settings, refunds, disputes, and processor relationship
with the seller. Gumroad bundles checkout, delivery,
merchant-of-record tax handling, and platform payout
rules into one system. Latuos sits between those options
by providing storefront and delivery while sellers use
their own Stripe account.

When does Gumroad still make more sense?

Gumroad still makes more sense if you want one system
that bundles checkout, merchant-of-record tax handling,
and Gumroad's payout rules, or if Discover matters to
your sales model. Selling direct on Stripe makes more
sense when keeping payments and payout visibility inside
your own Stripe account matters more than all-in-one
convenience.

Tired of Gumroad Fees? 5 Reasons Some Creators Consider
Leaving Gumroad

By Victor Organo, Founder of Latuos &middot; Updated June 16, 2026

Quick Answer

If you&#x27;re tired of Gumroad fees, the pain is usually
not just one line item. The 10% + $0.50 direct-sale cut gets
harder to ignore as volume grows, while payout timing and
merchant-of-record control still sit with Gumroad.

Some creators start considering alternatives when five
things stack up: the direct-sale math starts to feel
expensive, payout control stays with Gumroad, self-driven
sales make marketplace pricing harder to justify, platform
dependence can start to feel heavier, and disputes make the
tradeoff easier to notice. If you want the broader shortlist
after this page, use
Best Gumroad Alternatives in 2026 .

The 5 reasons

The 10% + $0.50 cut can stop feeling small.

Payout timing stays under Gumroad&#x27;s control.

Direct sellers may feel they are paying marketplace-style
pricing.

One platform account can start to feel like operational
dependency.

Refunds and disputes can make the tradeoff easier to
notice.

Reason 1: The 10% + $0.50 Cut Can Start Feeling Expensive

If most sales come through your own links or audience,
Gumroad&#x27;s published direct-sale fee is 10% + $0.50
before card or PayPal processing. That means $3.00 on a
$25 sale, $5.50 on a $50 sale, and $10.50 on a $100 sale
before processor fees.

That is usually when the search shifts from curiosity to
frustration. Many creators start asking why a self-driven
sale still carries marketplace-style pricing, and the fee
calculator makes the annual gap visible at their own price
point and volume.

Reason 2: Some Direct Sellers Revisit Marketplace Pricing

As sales grow, Gumroad&#x27;s setup convenience can start
to matter less than the fee drag built into customers you
already brought yourself. Email lists, repeat buyers, and
social traffic change the comparison because the seller is
no longer paying mostly for discovery.

Reason 3: Payout Timing Stays Under Gumroad&#x27;s Control

Some creators start thinking more about payout control
when they want more payout visibility, more operational
control, or fewer dependencies across the storefront and
payment workflow. That is when the merchant-of-record
model can feel more material to the decision.

Who this is best for

- Sellers already bringing their own traffic and
re-evaluating Gumroad’s tradeoffs.

- Sellers whose fee drag and payout dependence now feel
more important than convenience.

- Sellers deciding whether the business has moved beyond
the bundled-platform stage.

Who this is not for

- Sellers who still depend heavily on Gumroad Discover for
distribution.

- Sellers who have not validated demand yet and still
value maximum convenience.

- Sellers looking for one feature complaint rather than a
broader business decision.

Reason 4: Platform Dependence Can Matter More as Revenue
Grows

Many creators do not consider leaving Gumroad because one
feature suddenly disappears. For some sellers, the tipping
point is cumulative. Fee drag becomes more visible as
direct-sale volume rises. Payout control starts to matter
more once the revenue is no longer casual side income.
Platform dependency can also feel heavier once the
storefront becomes a real operating system for the
business.

That shift usually happens when the creator moves from the
convenience phase to the operating-business phase. Early
on, familiarity and fast setup can be worth a lot. Later,
clearer economics and lower dependency start to matter
more. The right comparison is not launch convenience in
isolation. It is what the business now needs from the
payment layer, delivery layer, and support workflow.

This helps explain why some creators consider leaving for
several reasons at once. Higher fees may be the visible
trigger. Payout dependence, platform dependency, and
wanting a more replaceable storefront are often part of
the evaluation underneath it.

Reason 5: Refunds and Disputes Can Clarify the Tradeoff

Refunds and disputes matter because they reveal whether
the same platform controls storefront access, payout
timing, and the payment record. On Gumroad, the platform
receives funds first and manages the merchant-of-record
workflow. In Stripe-direct setups, sellers see those
events in their own Stripe account and manage them there.
That does not make migration mandatory, but it often makes
the tradeoff harder to ignore once the business is mature
enough to care about operational control.

Frequently Asked Questions

Are fees the main reason creators leave Gumroad?

Fees are often the visible trigger, especially for
creators bringing their own traffic. For some sellers,
the deeper issue is how direct-sale fees combine with
payout dependence and platform dependency.

Does Gumroad take a cut of every sale?

Under Gumroad&#x27;s public pricing, direct or profile
sales are 10% + $0.50 per transaction and Discover sales
are 30% per transaction.

Why do some creators leave Gumroad?

Some creators revisit Gumroad once the fees, payout
control, and platform dependency start to matter more
than the convenience. Others still decide Gumroad is
worth keeping.

Do Creators Who Leave Gumroad Lose Their Existing
Customers?

Creators keep their audience, such as email lists and
social followers, but need to redirect product links.
Customer purchase history on Gumroad stays on Gumroad.

Is Gumroad still worth it if you bring your own traffic?

It can be worth it for fast setup or if Gumroad Discover
matters to you. If most buyers already come through your
own links, many creators decide the convenience no
longer justifies the fee and payout tradeoff.

Is Switching Platforms Disruptive?

For most digital product sellers, the switch takes a few
hours: recreate product pages, upload files, connect
Stripe, and update links. The disruption is usually
smaller than expected.

Sell Notion Templates: Fees & Platforms

By
Victor Organo, Founder of Latuos
&middot;
Updated July 12, 2026

Quick Answer

The best platform to sell Notion templates depends on
whether you already have an audience. Marketplaces like Etsy
and the Notion Marketplace win for discovery if you are
starting from zero. Your own Stripe checkout, through a tool
like Latuos, can be better for repeat revenue once you have
any traffic of your own.

Most Notion template listicles rank platforms in a single
flat list. That is the wrong shape for this decision. Etsy
and the Notion Marketplace exist because some creators need
discovery. Gumroad and your own checkout exist because other
creators need lower fees on traffic they already own. The
right platform changes as your audience grows.

Notion template pricing typically sits between $5 and $49.
At that range, platform fees eat into margin fast,
especially when a single creator sells multiple templates
across a growing catalog. This guide groups ten commonly
used options into three use-case segments so you can find
the platform that fits where you are right now, then upgrade
when your audience catches up.

If You Have No Audience Yet: Pick a Marketplace

With zero traffic, fees are not your problem. Discovery
is. A marketplace pays for the higher fee by sending
buyers who searched for Notion templates and would never
have found a personal storefront. Two platforms are worth
using here.

1. Etsy: Best for first sales without a following

Etsy is one of the strongest marketplace discovery options
for Notion templates today. Fees are 6.5% transaction +
$0.20 listing + country-dependent processing. The downside
is that Etsy gives you less control over customer
relationships and email list building than your own
checkout.

Best when: you have no list, no social following, and need
to validate whether anyone will buy your template at all.

2. Notion Marketplace: Best for reaching Notion users
inside Notion

The Notion Marketplace charges 10% + $0.40 per sale. The
audience is pre-qualified: every visitor is already a
Notion user. Notion is the merchant of record and pays out
biweekly after a 14-day eligibility hold.

Best when: your template targets serious Notion power
users who browse the in-product marketplace.

If You Have Some Audience: Use a Hybrid Setup

Once you have a small list, X / Twitter following, or any
repeat-visitor traffic, marketplace fees start to feel
expensive on the buyers you brought yourself. The hybrid
move is to keep a marketplace listing for discovery and
add a lower-fee storefront for direct traffic.

3. Gumroad: Best when you already have a Gumroad page

Gumroad is a common default for many working Notion
sellers. Direct sales are 10% + $0.50, but Discover
marketplace sales are 30%. Gumroad is the merchant of
record and pays out by direct deposit or PayPal, depending
on country.

Best when: you already have product pages, reviews, and
customers on Gumroad and switching cost is real.

4. Payhip: Best for free-tier validators

Payhip Free is 5% per sale with no monthly cost. Plus is
$29/month + 2%. Pro is $99/month + 0%. Seller connects
their own payment processor (Stripe, PayPal, or Paystack),
so you keep a clearer payment trail than with Gumroad.

Best when: you want a free entry point and a path to 0%
fees later without changing platforms.

5. Lemon Squeezy: Best when you do not want to handle VAT

Lemon Squeezy usually charges 5% + $0.50, with possible
additional payment, subscription, international, payout,
or marketing feature fees. It is the merchant of record,
which means they handle global sales tax and VAT for you.
Useful if you sell to EU buyers and do not want to manage
the tax filings yourself.

Best when: tax compliance is a bigger pain point than the
per-sale fee.

6. Ko-fi: Best for tip-jar style audiences

Ko-fi Free charges a 5% service fee on shop sales, plus
normal payment processing. Ko-fi Gold is $12/month with 0%
service fees if you turn off Contributor status. Suits
creators whose audience is used to pay-what-you-want or
low-priced templates.

Best when: your community already lives on Ko-fi and your
templates are priced under $20.

If You Have a List or Social Following: Own the Checkout

Once your traffic is mostly people you brought yourself,
marketplace fees are paying for discovery you do not need.
The math flips: a 3-5% checkout layer plus Stripe
processing usually keeps more revenue than a marketplace
take.

7. Latuos: Best for direct sales with your own Stripe
account

Latuos charges 2% + Stripe processing with no monthly fee.
Payments are created on the seller&#x27;s connected Stripe
account, so you stay merchant of record and Latuos does
not hold your seller balance or set your payout schedule.
Files (PDF, Notion duplication links, etc.) are delivered
after purchase.

Best when: you have direct traffic from Reddit, email,
YouTube, X / Twitter, or a personal site, and you want one
of the lowest percentage take-rates without a monthly
subscription.

See Latuos pricing &rarr;

See a real Latuos checkout for a Notion template
&rarr;

8. Sellfy: Best for storefront features at a monthly cost

Sellfy Starter starts at $22/month when billed annually,
or $29/month on monthly billing, with 0% Sellfy
transaction fees and a sales cap; higher tiers raise the
cap. Suits sellers who want print-on-demand bundles
alongside their Notion templates and prefer a subscription
over per-sale fees.

Best when: monthly volume justifies the subscription and
you want a full storefront builder.

9. Stan Store: Best for link-in-bio sellers

Stan Store Creator is $29/month, Creator Pro is $99/month,
with Stripe processing on top. Designed for creators
driving traffic from Instagram and TikTok bios. Limited as
a primary storefront but strong as a mobile checkout.

Best when: most of your traffic comes from a single
link-in-bio on social.

10. Whop: Best for community + template bundles

Whop&#x27;s total cost depends on the payment, tax,
affiliate, and marketplace features used. Its public docs
list orchestration at 0.8%, billing at 0.5%, tax and
remittance at 0.5%, and affiliate processing at 1.25%
where those tools apply. Suits creators bundling a
template with a paid community or Discord server.

Best when: the template is the front door to a paid
community, not the main product.

Watch: Selling a Notion Template with Your Own Stripe
Account

This 5-minute walkthrough shows the full flow on Latuos:
preparing a public Notion template link, creating the
product, publishing the buyer-facing page, completing a
test purchase, and confirming the payment in Stripe.

Fee Math: What Each Platform Keeps from a $29 Template

Notion templates typically price between $5 and $49. At a
$29 sale, here is what each headline platform fee takes
before taxes, payout costs, currency conversion, or
country-specific processing where applicable.

Gumroad direct platform fee: $3.40, before payment
processing

Gumroad Discover fee: $8.70

Notion Marketplace: $3.30

Payhip Free: $1.45

Lemon Squeezy: usually $1.95 before extra fees

Ko-fi Free shop sale: $1.45 + processing

Etsy (US): $2.09 + processing

Latuos: $0.58 (plus standard Stripe processing)

Across 50 sales a month, Gumroad&#x27;s direct platform
fee alone is roughly $1,008 a year higher than
Latuos&#x27;s 2% platform fee plus standard US Stripe
processing on the same $29 product, before Gumroad payment
processing, country-specific taxes, refunds, chargebacks,
or currency conversion. The
Fee Calculator
runs the math at your own price points across the full
catalog.

Why Notion Sellers Feel Fees Faster

Notion templates are often sold at lower prices and in
small bundles, which makes percentage fees more visible. A
creator with five templates priced between $9 and $29
feels the fee difference across the whole catalog, not
just on one product.

Notion sellers also tend to iterate products often. That
makes a simple storefront plus a seller-owned payment
account attractive because the catalog can change without
changing the payment layer.

Why Payout Control Still Matters for Notion Sellers

Notion creators often sell more than one template, so the
fee savings compound across a small catalog.

Many Notion sellers already use Gumroad, which makes the
migration story straightforward.

That difference shows up in refunds and disputes too. In a
direct Stripe setup, the seller has clearer ownership of
the payment trail and resolution flow. In a
marketplace-led setup, the platform shapes more of the
refund process, payout timing, and buyer support path.

Best for

Creators selling Notion templates to their own traffic and
wanting lower fees with payments routed to their own
Stripe account.

Not the best fit for

Sellers who want marketplace discovery, marketplace trust
signals, or a platform to sit between them and the
customer during checkout.

Frequently Asked Questions

What is the best platform to sell Notion templates in
2026?

It depends on your traffic source. If you have an
audience on social media, email, YouTube, or a personal
site, your own Stripe checkout (through a tool like
Latuos) usually keeps more revenue among the options
compared here. If you do not yet have an audience, Etsy
and the Notion Marketplace are two strong discovery
options.

Where do Notion template creators commonly sell?

Common platforms for Notion template sellers include
Gumroad, Etsy, the Notion Marketplace, and a
creator&#x27;s own storefront connected to Stripe. Many
sellers list on more than one to cover both direct
traffic and marketplace discovery.

Is selling Notion templates on Etsy worth it?

Etsy is worth it if you have no audience of your own and
need discovery. Etsy charges a 6.5% transaction fee, a
$0.20 listing fee, and country-dependent payment
processing. The discovery traffic offsets the fee for
sellers with no list or social following.

What does the Notion Marketplace charge to sell
templates?

The Notion Marketplace charges 10% plus $0.40 per sale
when using Notion Marketplace payments. Notion is the
merchant of record and pays out biweekly after a 14-day
eligibility hold.

Can I sell Notion templates with my own Stripe account?

Yes. Connect your own Stripe account to a storefront
layer like Latuos: you stay merchant of record, payments
are created on your connected Stripe account, and Latuos
does not hold your seller balance or set your payout
schedule. Latuos charges a 2% platform fee per sale with
no monthly subscription, and it delivers the template
link to the buyer automatically after checkout.

Gumroad alternative comparison

10 Gumroad Alternatives Compared: MoR vs Stripe-First

By Victor Organo, Founder of Latuos &middot; Updated July 1, 2026

Quick Answer

The most important question when leaving Gumroad is not only
which platform has the lowest fee. It is whether you want a
merchant-of-record platform that handles the payment and tax
relationship, or a Stripe-first setup where the seller keeps
the payment account and the tax responsibility.

Gumroad and Lemon Squeezy explicitly claim
merchant-of-record status. Latuos sits in the opposite lane:
sellers connect their own Stripe account, pay a 2% Latuos
platform fee plus Stripe processing, and remain responsible
for merchant-of-record obligations.

Grouped by status from the table below: sellers stay
merchant of record on Latuos, Sellfy, and SendOwl, each of
which confirms the seller's own Stripe or PayPal account
handles the sale. Gumroad and Lemon Squeezy are the
platform-claimed merchant-of-record options. Payhip uses
seller-of-record wording rather than the MoR term, Whop is
conditional because its treatment varies by tax mode and
payment flow, and Stan Store, Podia, Teachable, and Patreon
are not publicly confirmed either way.

Lowest no-monthly fee

Latuos

2% platform fee plus Stripe processing.

Best free-start all-rounder

Payhip

Free plan, upgrade path, and connected payout processors.

Best software fit

Lemon Squeezy

5% + $0.50 with merchant-of-record tax handling.

Best course-first fits

Podia / Teachable

Better matches when the business is course-led, not
checkout-led.

Best-fit labels on this page are our editorial judgment based
on public pricing, payout setup, and product focus. Pricing
claims use US public pricing checked against official public
sources on June 26, 2026.

What "merchant of record" changes for digital sellers

Stripe's public resource on
merchant of record vs. seller of record
frames the practical distinction around who is legally
responsible for the transaction at the point of sale. For a
creator leaving Gumroad, that distinction matters as much as
headline platform fees.

When a platform is
merchant of record , it stays in the payment path, handles the buyer-facing
sale, collects and remits applicable taxes where it claims
that role, manages receipts, and pays the seller from the
platform's payout system. Gumroad's pricing page says it is
merchant of record as of January 1, 2025. Lemon Squeezy's
public docs also state a merchant-of-record role.

When the
seller owns the payment account , the seller's processor setup controls the transaction
record, payout timing, refunds, and disputes. In a
Stripe-first setup like Latuos, the seller connects their
own Stripe account and stays responsible for tax and
compliance obligations rather than outsourcing them to an
MoR platform.

Neither model is inherently better. MoR platforms can reduce
tax administration work. Stripe-first platforms can give the
seller more direct payment visibility and payout control.
This page compares the trade-off platform by platform rather
than assuming every Gumroad replacement solves the same
problem.

Which responsibilities do you want to own?

Payments through a platform balance, or through your own
payment account?

Refunds and disputes in a platform flow, or inside your
processor dashboard?

Tax and VAT handled by an MoR platform, or managed by the
seller with appropriate advice?

Payout timing governed by the platform, or by your
processor account?

MoR status and payout control compared

Gumroad is included in the table below as the baseline so
the alternatives are easier to judge. "Best for" is an
editorial label, not an official platform claim. Where a
merchant-of-record status or payout setup is not clearly
stated on official public pricing or help pages, the table
says so instead of guessing.

claim. Payhip is described as seller-of-record and

The best Gumroad alternatives in 2026

The platforms below are not interchangeable. Some are best
thought of as direct-sale storefronts, some as course tools,
some as community or membership platforms, and some as
merchant-of-record software infrastructure. The "best for"
judgment in each review is our read of the public pricing,
payout setup, and product focus - not a copy of each
platform's own marketing.

1. Latuos - Best for sellers who want to stay on Stripe

Latuos is the clearest fit when the business is one-time
digital products sold to traffic you already own. Public
Latuos pricing is simple: 2% platform fee, no monthly
fee, and separate Stripe processing. The seller remains
responsible as merchant of record via their own Stripe
setup, and payments land in the seller's own connected
Stripe account.

Why it fits:
If your main question is "how do I lower direct-sale fee
drag without adding a monthly subscription or giving up
control of the payment account?" Latuos is built for
exactly that. The model is especially strong for ebooks,
PDFs, guides, templates, digital art, and other one-time
files where Gumroad's 10% + $0.50 direct-sale fee
compounds fast.

Payout model:
Refunds, disputes, and payouts stay tied to Stripe
rather than a marketplace balance. That is a structural
difference, not just a pricing difference. For a closer
look at that operational tradeoff, see
Stripe payout ownership explained .

Main tradeoff:
Latuos is not a marketplace, does not currently support
subscriptions, and seller onboarding is currently
limited to businesses in the US, CA, GB, AU, NZ, and SG.
If you have already narrowed the decision to these two
tools, use
Gumroad vs Latuos
instead of this broader roundup.

2. Payhip - Best free-start all-rounder

Payhip remains one of the most credible broad Gumroad
alternatives because the pricing ladder is easy to
understand. Public pricing is 5% on the free plan,
$29/month + 2% on Plus, and $99/month + 0% on Pro.
Payouts go through connected payment processors, with
Payhip's public pricing emphasizing PayPal and Stripe.

Why it fits:
Payhip is the practical answer for creators who want a
real free-start option but are not sure yet whether they
will stay on a percentage-of-sales plan or move to a
fixed monthly plan later. It keeps the commitment low
while still offering a recognizable upgrade path. The
detailed
Payhip vs Latuos fee breakdown
covers the direct-sale math side by side.

Payout model:
Public Payhip materials present it as a
connected-processor and seller-of-record setup rather
than a merchant-of-record replacement. This page uses
that wording instead of claiming a full MoR role.

Main tradeoff:
On direct sales, Payhip Free still costs more per sale
than Latuos, and once you reach Plus or Pro you need to
justify a monthly bill. It is a broader all-rounder than
a pure low-fee winner.

3. Lemon Squeezy - Best for software and SaaS

Lemon Squeezy is usually the cleanest answer when the
product is software, SaaS, or digital goods sold
globally with tax complexity attached. Public pricing
and fee docs currently show 5% + $0.50, and Lemon
Squeezy publicly positions itself as the merchant of
record that handles global VAT and tax.

Why it fits:
If global tax handling, software billing, and a
merchant-of-record model are more important than keeping
the sale inside your own Stripe account, Lemon Squeezy
is easier to justify than forcing a direct Stripe
storefront into a software role it was not built for.
The detailed
Lemon Squeezy vs Latuos comparison
covers the fee and payout trade-offs side by side.

Payout model:
Lemon Squeezy stays in the payment path and pays out on
its own schedule. That is the flip side of the tax and
compliance convenience.

Main tradeoff:
This is not the better fit if your priority is owning
the Stripe account connected to your sales. Its value is
that it removes that responsibility, not that it keeps
it with you.

4. Sellfy - Best if you prefer fixed monthly pricing

Sellfy is the fixed-fee alternative for sellers who want
to get away from revenue share once they have
predictable sales volume. Public pricing shows Starter
at $29/month, Business at $79/month, and Premium at
$159/month, with no Sellfy transaction fee on paid
plans.

Why it fits:
Sellfy becomes easier to justify when your objection to
Gumroad is not just "the percentage is high" but
"software cost should stop growing directly with my
revenue." That is a very different economic philosophy
from a pure percentage-based platform.

Payout model:
Public Sellfy docs frame payments as going to the
seller's Stripe or PayPal account and state that the
seller is merchant of record, while Sellfy is not.

Main tradeoff:
There is no true free plan, and Sellfy's public pricing
also references a 2% overage fee if you exceed a plan
cap without upgrading. It is strongest once your monthly
volume is stable enough to justify software spend. The
Sellfy vs Latuos comparison
covers where the fixed-monthly model starts to make
sense.

5. Stan Store - Best for link-in-bio creators

Stan Store's public plan pricing is simple: $29/month
for Creator and $99/month for Creator Pro, with public
help docs describing 0% Stan transaction fees. Payments
are handled through processor-linked Stripe or PayPal
setup, with processor fees applying separately.

Why it fits:
Stan Store makes the most sense when the business is
social-led and link-in-bio driven. If the bulk of your
traffic lives on Instagram, TikTok, or short-form video,
a tool optimized for that flow can matter more than a
lower platform percentage on paper. The
Stan Store vs Latuos comparison
covers this link-in-bio trade-off.

Payout model:
Public docs describe processor-linked payments, but they
do not clearly state merchant-of-record status. Stan's
Stripe setup is also not the same as a normal
seller-owned Stripe account, so this page avoids that
claim.

Main tradeoff:
Stan Store is a fixed monthly commitment before the
first sale, so it is harder to justify for creators who
mainly want lower-fee download checkout without ongoing
software overhead.

6. Podia - Best for courses plus downloads

Podia sits in the middle ground between a download
storefront and a course platform. Public pricing
currently shows yearly-billed monthly equivalents: Mover
at $42/month with a 5% fee, Shaker at $84/month with a
0% fee, and Earthquaker at $150/month with a 0% fee.
Payments run through the seller's Stripe or PayPal
accounts.

Why it fits:
Based on public pricing and product positioning, Podia
is strongest when you are not just selling files. It is
more attractive when the catalog mixes courses,
downloads, and recurring offers and you want one tool to
cover that creator-business mix.

Payout model:
Podia publicly presents direct payment processor
connections, but merchant-of-record status is not made
explicit on public pricing, so this page does not claim
it one way or the other.

Main tradeoff:
Podia is harder to justify if the only job you need done
is low-cost one-time digital checkout. In that scenario,
the fixed monthly fee can be the wrong part of the
stack.

7. Teachable - Best for course-first businesses

Teachable is the course-first alternative in this list.
Public pricing shows Starter at $39/month with a 7.5%
fee, Builder at $89/month with 0% transaction fees, and
Growth at $189/month with 0% transaction fees. Teachable
also publicly lists global payments and tax features, so
its payout and tax behavior should be checked against
the payment setup you choose.

Why it fits:
If the product is primarily a course business, it makes
more sense to compare Gumroad against course-native
tools than against pure download storefronts. Teachable
belongs in that narrower comparison, not because it is
the cheapest, but because it is built around the course
workflow.

Payout model:
Teachable's payout behavior depends on the payment setup
you use, which is why its economics are not as simple as
a single Stripe-first direct-sales tool.

Main tradeoff:
Teachable is a weak fit if your business is mostly
low-ticket one-time downloads. The Starter plan combines
a monthly bill with a transaction fee, which is hard to
justify for lightweight direct sales.

8. Whop - Best for community and access products

Whop's public pricing starts at 2.7% + $0.30 for
domestic card transactions with no monthly fee to start.
It is one of the more attractive numbers in the roundup
if you only look at the headline rate, but Whop's public
pricing is layered and payout details vary by payment
type, payout method, and enabled services. The
Whop fees breakdown
covers that variability.

Why it fits:
Whop is strongest when the product is community access,
memberships, or gated digital access rather than a
classic storefront for standalone downloads. In that
kind of business, checkout is only part of the value.

Payout model:
Because Whop's merchant-of-record treatment is limited
and can vary by tax mode and payment flow, Whop is not
as simple to evaluate on payout ownership as a plain
Stripe-first download platform.

Main tradeoff:
Whop is not the obvious fit if all you need is a clean
storefront for ebooks, PDFs, or one-time files. Its
strength is access products and community commerce.

9. Patreon - Best for recurring fan support

Patreon is the recurring-membership option in this
comparison, not a direct substitute for every Gumroad
store. Patreon's current standard fee for new creators
is 10% of income, while some legacy creators may be on
older fee structures. Patreon publicly states that it
handles tax collection. Payouts run through Patreon
rather than a direct Stripe-owned storefront model.

Why it fits:
If the product is ongoing fan support, membership,
behind-the-scenes access, or recurring creator revenue,
Patreon fits that model more naturally than a one-time
download storefront ever will.

Payout model:
This is a platform-managed payout path, not a direct
Stripe-account ownership story.

Main tradeoff:
Patreon is usually the wrong fit if you are mainly
trying to lower the cost of selling one-off digital
downloads. It solves a different revenue model.

10. SendOwl - Best for sellers who want software pricing
over revenue share

SendOwl's public pricing is monthly software pricing,
not a take-rate model. Public pricing shows Starter at
$39/month, Growth at $87/month, Pro at $159/month, and
Business from $299/month. That makes it the classic
"software subscription instead of revenue share"
alternative.

Why it fits:
If your sales volume is predictable and you want a fixed
software cost instead of a growing platform cut, SendOwl
belongs on the shortlist. It is most useful once the
business is mature enough to prefer a known monthly bill
to a percentage of every sale.

Payout model:
SendOwl's public help states that payments happen
through the seller's own payment gateway account and
that the seller is merchant of record. This makes it
closer to a seller-controlled payment model than a
merchant-of-record platform.

Main tradeoff:
There is no true free selling plan beyond trial or
signup language, and the public pricing page is less
explicit about payout structure than some of the other
tools in this roundup. If payout ownership matters to
you, verify it before switching.

Which Gumroad alternative is right for you?

If you want a free Gumroad alternative

Start with Latuos, Payhip Free, Whop, or Lemon Squeezy.
Those are the no-monthly-fee options in this roundup. The
important caveat is that "free" only means $0 monthly. It
does not mean $0 per sale. If your product is a one-time
download and your traffic is already yours, Latuos is the
cleanest low-fee answer at 2% plus Stripe processing. If
you want a broader all-rounder that can stay free and
still upgrade later, Payhip is the other obvious starting
point. For a wider view, see
platforms with no monthly fee .

If fees are starting to drag on direct sales

Once you already bring your own traffic, Gumroad's direct
pricing becomes easier to question. That is where a
direct-sale alternative like Latuos or Payhip usually
beats staying on Gumroad just for convenience. If you have
already narrowed your decision down to one direct Stripe
option, use
Gumroad vs Latuos
for the shorter side-by-side decision.

The mistake here is comparing only headline percentages.
Gumroad direct pricing includes a fixed $0.50 fee on every
direct sale. At a $25 average order value, that fixed fee
alone adds $10 per 20 orders. Fixed monthly tools remove
that fee but add software cost before the first sale. The
right answer is the one that matches both your average
order size and your volume. Use the
fee calculator
to run your own price point.

If keeping your own Stripe account matters

The strongest shortlist is Latuos, Payhip, Sellfy, and
Podia. Stan Store also uses a processor-linked setup, but
its Stripe flow should not be treated as a normal
seller-owned Stripe account without checking the details.
That does not make connected-processor platforms
automatically better. It simply means they align better
with the seller-controlled payout model many Gumroad
switchers are looking for. If that is the real tradeoff
you are making, use
platforms with direct creator payouts
for the cleaner side-by-side.

If you sell courses or memberships

Podia and Teachable are the more natural course-first
options here. Patreon and Whop are stronger when the core
product is recurring access rather than one-time files. If
the product model is not really "downloads sold through a
simple storefront," it is usually a mistake to optimize
only for the lowest checkout fee.

If you sell software or want merchant-of-record tax
handling

Lemon Squeezy is usually the clearest fit. Gumroad itself
also sits on the merchant-of-record side of the market,
but Lemon Squeezy is the alternative in this roundup that
most directly targets software and SaaS needs. The
tradeoff is straightforward: more built-in tax handling,
less direct ownership of the payment account.

How to switch from Gumroad without breaking your workflow

Switching platforms is not only a content move. It is also a
payout-model decision. Before you migrate, decide whether
you want a direct Stripe setup, a fixed-fee software tool,
or a merchant-of-record platform that stays between you and
the payment account.

Understand what changes when you leave an MoR platform

Moving away from Gumroad means moving away from its
merchant-of-record layer for new sales. On a Stripe-first
setup, tax and VAT collection, receipts, refunds,
disputes, and payout visibility move closer to your own
payment account.

Orders placed through Gumroad before migration remain part
of Gumroad's historical transaction flow. New orders
placed through your replacement platform follow that
platform's payment model. If you move to Latuos, refunds
and disputes are handled through the seller's Stripe
account because buyers pay that connected account
directly.

This page is not legal or tax advice. If MoR tax handling
is one of the reasons you use Gumroad today, verify your
obligations with a qualified adviser before switching to a
seller-controlled Stripe setup.

1. Pick the replacement model first

Decide whether you want to keep payments in your own
Stripe account, move to a flat monthly software tool, or
use a merchant-of-record platform that handles tax and
payout flow for you.

2. Audit the catalog and customer records you need

Save the product copy, files, pricing, customer records,
and any support context you want to keep before you start
rebuilding pages elsewhere. Export what Gumroad allows
before old links are retired.

3. Rebuild the products and replace the links you control

Recreate product pages, test checkout and delivery, and
then replace the links on your website, bio, email
sequences, pinned posts, and other channels you own. Make
the replacement only after confirmation emails and receipt
behavior are correct.

4. Treat traffic sources separately from the catalog move

Tell your email list and relevant social channels where to
buy after the switch. Keep support language clear for
legacy Gumroad purchases versus new purchases on the
replacement platform.

Most migration mistakes happen when creators think only
about product pages and forget everything around them:
payout model, tax handling, public links, email flows, old
receipts, and customer expectations. If your decision is
still mostly about the practical detail for template
sellers, read
migrating Notion templates from Gumroad . If your decision is still mostly about the economic
reason for leaving, read
Why creators leave Gumroad
before you start moving files.

Compare the finalists at your price

If this roundup has narrowed your shortlist, use the direct
comparison tools next. The fastest path is usually: exact
Gumroad fee breakdown, side-by-side Gumroad vs Latuos, then
the fee calculator at your own price point.

Frequently asked questions

Is Gumroad a merchant of record?

Yes. Gumroad's public pricing page explicitly states that
Gumroad is merchant of record and says this applies since
January 1, 2025. This page treats that as Gumroad's own
public claim.

Does Lemon Squeezy handle VAT as merchant of record?

Yes, based on Lemon Squeezy's own public documentation.
Lemon Squeezy states that it is merchant of record for
sales through its platform and publicly markets global
sales tax and compliance handling.

Do I still have to handle taxes if I move to a
Stripe-first platform?

Generally, yes. When you move from an MoR platform to a
Stripe-first setup, tax and VAT obligations that were
handled by the platform can become the seller's
responsibility. This page is not tax advice; verify
obligations with a qualified adviser.

Can I keep my own Stripe account connected to a
storefront?

Yes. Latuos is designed for this model: the seller
connects their own Stripe account, payments flow to that
account, and refunds, disputes, and payouts stay visible
in Stripe. Other platforms may support processor-connected
payments, but the exact Stripe ownership model can vary.

What happens to refunds and disputes after migration?

Purchases made on Gumroad before migration remain part of
Gumroad's historical transaction flow. New purchases
follow the replacement platform's model. On a Stripe-first
platform like Latuos, refunds and disputes are handled
through the seller's Stripe account.

What is the cheapest Gumroad alternative with no monthly
fee?

For one-time digital products, the lowest no-monthly-fee
platform fee in this comparison is Latuos at 2% plus
separate Stripe processing. Payhip Free is 5%, Whop starts
at 2.7% + $0.30, and Lemon Squeezy is 5% + $0.50.

The catch is that "cheapest" depends on what you actually
need. The cheapest raw fee is not automatically the best
answer if you also need merchant-of-record tax handling,
course tools, or a community product.

Is there a free Gumroad alternative that still lets you
keep your own Stripe account?

Yes. Latuos charges no monthly fee and uses the seller's
own Stripe account. Payhip also offers a no-monthly-fee
plan and routes payouts through connected payment
processors.

That makes both platforms more relevant than a
merchant-of-record alternative if your priority is payout
ownership, not tax outsourcing.

Which Gumroad alternatives handle tax or VAT for you?

Gumroad and Lemon Squeezy explicitly state
merchant-of-record tax handling. Payhip also says it
collects and remits EU VAT and UK tax automatically, but
this page does not treat Payhip as a merchant-of-record
platform because Payhip uses seller-of-record wording
rather than a clear universal MoR claim. Patreon publicly
states tax collection handling, but this page does not
treat that alone as a full merchant-of-record claim.

Latuos does not take over the merchant-of-record role. The
seller keeps the Stripe account and remains responsible
for their own tax setup and compliance obligations.

Which Gumroad alternatives let you keep your own Stripe
account?

In this roundup, Latuos, Payhip, Sellfy, and Podia are the
strongest connected-processor shortlist. Stan Store uses
processor-linked payments, but its Stripe setup should not
be treated as a normal seller-owned Stripe account without
checking the details.

Teachable has multiple payout paths, so if keeping your
own Stripe account is a hard requirement you should verify
the exact payment setup before switching.

Which Gumroad alternative is best for software or SaaS?

Based on the public pricing and merchant-of-record setup,
Lemon Squeezy is usually the clearest fit for software and
SaaS sellers who want tax handling built in.

It becomes a weaker fit if your main goal is keeping the
payment account inside your own Stripe dashboard.

Which Gumroad alternative is best for courses?

Podia and Teachable are usually the strongest course-first
options in this roundup. They are not necessarily the
lowest-cost options, but they make more sense than a pure
download storefront when courses are the main business.

Which Gumroad alternative is best for memberships?

Patreon, Whop, and Podia are usually the better starting
points when the product is recurring membership access.
Patreon is strongest for fan support, Whop for community
and access products, and Podia for a creator business that
mixes memberships with downloads or courses.

Can you move off Gumroad without losing your audience?

Usually yes. You still own your website, social links, and
email list, but you need to rebuild product pages, replace
the links you control, and decide how to handle legacy
customers.

The bigger risk is not the move itself. It is moving
without a plan for links, customer communication, and any
traffic source you were getting through Gumroad.

Does Latuos replace Gumroad for every use case?

No. Latuos is strongest for one-time digital products sold
to direct traffic, with payments tied to the seller's own
Stripe account.

It is weaker if you want marketplace discovery,
merchant-of-record tax handling, or subscriptions and
memberships.

Does Gumroad still make sense for some creators?

Yes. Gumroad can still make sense if you value its
built-in marketplace, a merchant-of-record setup, and a
bundled tax layer more than you value lower direct-sale
fees or owning the Stripe account connected to your sales.

About this comparison

This page is intentionally first-party. We run Latuos, so
the goal is not to pretend neutrality. The goal is to make
the tradeoffs explicit enough that the page is still useful
even when the best answer is not Latuos.

All pricing claims use US public pricing checked on June 26,
2026. "Best for" labels are editorial judgments based on
those public pricing pages, payout setup, and product focus.
When a merchant-of-record status or payout ownership was not
clearly stated on the public source, this page left the
claim unconfirmed rather than filling the gap with
guesswork. That matters more than sounding cleaner, because
payout and tax claims are exactly where comparison pages go
stale.

Fee data for all platforms, with per-platform verification
dates and a fee change log, lives in the
Platform Fees Index .

Platform comparison for digital artists

Where Digital Artists Keep More of Each Sale

By Victor Organo, Founder of Latuos &middot; Updated June 16, 2026

Quick Answer

Digital art sellers often end up running two models:
marketplace discovery and direct audience sales. The
platform choice changes depending on which side of the
business you are optimizing.

If you need marketplace discovery, Etsy is still a strong
option for digital art. If you already have an audience and
want lower fees with direct Stripe payouts, Latuos is the
stronger direct-sales setup.

Digital art sellers often split between marketplaces for
discovery and standalone stores for direct sales. The right
setup depends on how much of your traffic comes from your
own audience versus platform search.
